Hodge Operators and Exceptional Isomorphisms between Unitary Groups
Linus Kramer, Markus J. Stroppel

Abstract
We give a generalization of the Hodge operator to spaces ( V , h ) (V,h) endowed with a hermitian or symmetric bilinear form h h over arbitrary fields, including the characteristic two case. Suitable exterior powers of V V become free modules over an algebra K K defined using such an operator. This leads to several exceptional homomorphisms from unitary groups (with respect to h h ) into groups of semi-similitudes with respect to a suitable form over some subfield of K K . The algebra K K depends on h h ; it is a composition algebra unless h h is symmetric and the characteristic is two.



Mathematics Subject Classification:

20G15

20E32

20G20

20G40

22C05

11E39

11E57

Keywords:
hermitian form, symmetric bilinear form, exterior product,
Pfaffian form, Hodge operator, exceptional isomorphism,
composition algebra, quaternion algebra

Introduction

In many branches of mathematics, attempts at a systematic study (or
even a classification) of certain objects lead to the consideration of
families of examples constructed from given data (such as a
ground field, dimension, an invariant form, …).
With suitably defined categories, these constructions may even be
considered as functors. The reader may think of classical groups (data comprise a field, a
dimension, and an invariant form), or Dynkin diagrams (data comprise a
letter indicating a construction of a graph, and a
natural number giving the number of vertices in that graph),
or split forms of simple Lie algebras (data comprise a field, and a
Dynkin diagram),
or finite simple groups of Lie type (data again comprise a field, and a
Dynkin diagram),
or classical polar spaces (data comprise a field, a dimension, and a
polarity of the projective space of that dimension over that field).

While these functors usually assign non-isomorphic structures to
different data sets, it often happens that unexpected isomorphisms
occur for “small” data sets.
If such an exceptional isomorphism occurs, it usually induces an
exceptional situation in a related field, or can be explained and
understood by such a situation.

In the present paper, we are interested in exceptional isomorphisms
between certain classical groups. In this context, the simple groups
in question are usually obtained by forming first the commutator group
and then its quotient by the center, while the natural habitats of
classical groups are inside linear groups. For that reason, it is
sometimes better to study not only isomorphisms but also
homomorphisms.

We show that certain homomorphisms between classical groups can be
explained by variants of the Hodge operator. Some of the isomorphisms
discussed here are not easily accessible by other methods, so we think
our considerations are worth the effort. We have tried to keep the presentation reasonably self-contained, in
particular, we avoid the use of elaborate results from Lie theory or
the theory of algebraic groups. Applications to real Lie groups are
made explicit in several examples (see
2.14
,
3.10
,

4.3
,
4.4
,
5.9
,

5.10
, and
7.8
); applications to finite groups are explicit
in
5.14
, and
7.9
.

1
The Hodge operator

1.1 Notation
.

We consider a field
{\boldsymbol{F}}
of arbitrary characteristic and a field
automorphism
\sigma\colon x\mapsto{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
whose square is the
identity. Let
{\boldsymbol{R}}
denote the fixed field of
\sigma
. So either

{\boldsymbol{F}}={\boldsymbol{R}}
or
{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}
is a Galois extension of degree
2
(by Artin’s
Theorem, see
[
25
, VI, Th. 1.8]
). In any case we call
N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}\colon{\boldsymbol{F}}\to{\boldsymbol{R}}\colon x\mapsto{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}x

the
norm form
of
{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}
. Note that
N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}({\boldsymbol{F}})
is just
the set
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.21pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\displaystyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\textstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\scriptstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.6pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.18pt\hbox{$\kern-1.6pt\scriptscriptstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.5pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{x^{2}}\left|\vphantom{x^{2}x\in{\boldsymbol{F}}}\right.\,{x\in{\boldsymbol{F}}}\right\}
of squares if

\sigma=\mathrm{id}
.

A map
\lambda\colon V\to W
between vector space over
{\boldsymbol{F}}
is
called
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-semilinear
if
\lambda
is additive and there
exists an automorphism
\varphi
of
{\boldsymbol{F}}
(called the companion
of
\lambda
) such that
\lambda(sv)=\varphi(s)\lambda(v)
holds
for each
s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
and each
v\in V
. If we want to state explicitly
that the companion is
\varphi
, we speak of an

{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\varphi
-semilinear map. (In the context of real and complex analysis, one encounters

\mathbb{C}
-

-semilinear maps under the name

\mathbb{C}
-antilinear maps.)

Let
(V,h)
be an
n
-dimensional non-degenerate
\sigma
-hermitian
space over
{\boldsymbol{F}}
; i.e., a vector space
V
over
F
with a
bi-additive map
h\colon V\times V\to F
such that

h(v,ws)=h(v,w)s
and
h(w,v)={\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{h(v,w)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{h(v,w)}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{h(v,w)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{h(v,w)}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
hold for all

v,w\in V
and each
s\in F
. (In particular, the map
h
is
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-linear in the right variable,
and
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\sigma
-semilinear in the left variable.)

If
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
then the form
h
is a symmetric bilinear form. Such
forms occur as polarizations of quadratic forms; i.e., as

f(x,y)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q(x+y)-q(x)-q(y)
for a quadratic form
q
. (Some
authors refer to
f
as the polar form associated with
q
.) For any quadratic form in characteristic two, the polarization is an
alternating form.

If
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
and
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2
we also assume that
h
is
diagonalizable, i.e. that there exists an orthogonal basis. We note that such a form will not occur as polarization of a
quadratic form (unless it is zero). In particular, the orthogonal group with respect to a

bilinear
form on
\mathbb{F}_{2^{e}}^{n}
differs from the group usually
denoted by
\mathrm{O\kern 0.0pt}^{\varepsilon}(n,2^{e})
. (If
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}\neq 2
or
\sigma\neq\mathrm{id}
then diagonalizability is no
extra condition, cf.
[
9
, §8, p. 15]

or
[
28
, I.3.4]
.) A vector
v\in V\smallsetminus\{0\}
is called
isotropic

(with respect to
h
) if
h(v,v)=0
; the form
h
is called
isotropic if there exists an isotropic vector.

As we are going to extend the field
{\boldsymbol{F}}
to a non-commutative
composition algebra (containing
{\boldsymbol{F}}
as a non-central subalgebra)
over
{\boldsymbol{R}}
, we have to be precise: elements of
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{n}
will be
considered as columns, with scalars acting from the right, and
matrices acting from the left. However, elements of
{\boldsymbol{R}}
(in
particular, signs such as
(-1)^{m}
) will also occur on the left.

As
\dim{V}
is assumed to be finite, our assumption that
h
be not
degenerate is equivalent to the fact that we obtain an

{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\sigma
-semilinear isomorphism onto the dual space
V^{\vee}
,
namely

h^{\vee}\colon V\to V^{\vee}\colon v\mapsto h(v,-)\,

see e.g.
[
22
, Ch. I, §2]
. Consider now the exterior
algebra
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}
, cf.
[
20
, VI 9]
. We note
that
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{}
is a functor on vector spaces and semilinear maps,
cf.
1.6
below. Moreover, there is a natural
isomorphism
\textstyle({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V})^{\vee}\cong{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}(V^{\vee})\,,
so we may write unambiguously
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}^{\vee}
. Explicitly, we have
\langle f_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge f_{\ell},w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{\ell}\rangle=\det(\langle f_{i},w_{j}\rangle)
for
f_{i}\in V^{\vee}
and
w_{j}\in V
, see
[
28
, I.5.6]

or
1
1

1

The treatment in
[
5
]
is quite different from that in
later editions
[
7
]
,
[
8
]
.

[
5
, § 8, Thme. 1, p. 102]
; here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the natural pairing between a
vector space and its dual. Applying the functor
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}
to
h^{\vee}\colon V\to V^{\vee}
, we
obtain
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h^{\vee}}\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}\to{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V^{\vee}}
; we interpret
this as a hermitian form
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h}
on the exterior
algebra
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}
. Using the explicit formula above, we find

\textstyle{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h}(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell},w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{\ell})={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell},w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{\ell})=\det(h(v_{i},w_{j})).

1.2 The Pfaffian form
.

The exterior algebra comes with a natural
\mathbb{Z}
-grading and

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}V
is
1
-dimensional. We fix an
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-linear isomorphism

b\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}V\to{\boldsymbol{F}}
. For each positive integer
\ell\leq n
, the
map
b
then induces an
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-linear isomorphism

\mathrm{Pf}\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}V\to{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V^{\vee}
given by

\textstyle\mathrm{Pf}(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n-\ell})(w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{\ell})=b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n-\ell}\wedge w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{\ell})\,.

This is the
Pfaffian form
, see
[
20
, VI 10 Problems 23–28,
VIII 12 Problem 42]
. The resulting bilinear map
\mathrm{Pf}

on
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}
is “graded symmetric”,

\textstyle\mathrm{Pf}(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n-\ell},w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{\ell})=(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\,\mathrm{Pf}(w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{\ell},v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n-\ell})\,.

If
n=2\ell
then the bilinear form
\mathrm{Pf}
is isotropic on

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
; in fact, we then have

\mathrm{Pf}(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell},v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell})=0
for each basic
\ell
-vector
v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell}
.

1.3 Remark
.

For
n=4
and
\ell=2
we are dealing with the space
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!2}}{{\boldsymbol{F}}^{4}}

that carries the Klein quadric. The
quadratic form

\mathrm{Pq}

defining the Klein quadric is also referred to as a Pfaffian form
(cf.
[
13
]
and
[
30
]
where this form is
denoted by
q
), and
\mathrm{Pf}
is the symmetric bilinear form
obtained as polarization of that quadratic form
\mathrm{Pq}
. This is no
source of confusion as long as
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}\neq 2
. However, the polar
form
\mathrm{Pf}
carries less information than the quadratic form
\mathrm{Pq}
if

\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2
.

If one interprets the elements of
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!2}}{{\boldsymbol{F}}^{4}}
as alternating
matrices then there exists a scalar
s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}
such that

\mathrm{Pq}(X)^{2}=s\,\det{X}
holds for each
X\in{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!2}}{{\boldsymbol{F}}^{4}}
,
cf.
[
6
, § 5 no. 2, Prop. 2, p. 84]
; the scalar
s

reflects the choice of basis underlying that interpretation.
See
[
32
, 12.14]
for an interpretation of
\mathrm{Pq}
in
terms of the exterior algebra.

1.4 The Hodge operator
.

We now consider the composite

J\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{Pf}^{-1}\circ{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 13.36807pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.36807pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.99947pt\raise 10.43062pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.5695pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.21532pt\raise-6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.36812pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.36812pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.91649pt\raise-6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.4824pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Pf}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 105.9932pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 105.9932pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}V\,.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

This
\sigma
-linear isomorphism is the
Hodge operator
. It
depends, of course, on
h
and on
b
but not on the choice of basis.
See, e.g.,
[
27
, pp. 21–31]
for a discussion of the Hodge
operator for euclidean spaces over
\mathbb{R}
.

1.5 Explicit computation
.

Suppose that
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
is an orthogonal
2
2

2

If
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2
and
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
then an orthogonal basis exists by
our diagonalizability
assumption.
basis of
V
. For
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}
we use the basis vectors

v_{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{i_{\ell}}
with ascending

i_{1}<\dots<i_{\ell}\leq n
. Then
{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell},-)}
is a linear form on
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}
which annihilates each one of
those basis vectors, except for
v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell}
; in fact

\textstyle{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell},v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell})=h(v_{1},v_{1})\cdots h(v_{\ell},v_{\ell})\,.

In other words:
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}
is again diagonalizable, and so
is
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h}
. It then also follows that both

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}h\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}\times{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}\to{\boldsymbol{F}}
and

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h}\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}\times{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}\to{\boldsymbol{F}}
are not degenerate. The linear form
\mathrm{Pf}(v_{\ell+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})
annihilates
the same collection of basis
\ell
-vectors, and

\mathrm{Pf}(v_{\ell+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n},v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell})=(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\,b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle J(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{\ell+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}\,\frac{h(v_{1},v_{1})\cdots h(v_{\ell},v_{\ell})}{b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})}\,(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\,.

Similarly, we compute

\displaystyle J(v_{\ell+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell}\,\frac{h(v_{\ell+1},v_{\ell+1})\cdots h(v_{n},v_{n})}{b(v_{\ell+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}\wedge v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell})}\,(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\ell}\,\frac{h(v_{\ell+1},v_{\ell+1})\cdots h(v_{n},v_{n})}{b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})}\,.

Note that these formulae are correct only if
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
is an
orthogonal basis, and cannot be used if
v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{\ell}

corresponds to a subspace
U
of
V
such that
h|_{U\times U}
is
degenerate.

1.6 Functoriality
.

It is well known that
\bigotimes^{k}\colon V\mapsto\bigotimes^{k}V

and
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!k}}\colon V\mapsto{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!k}}{V}
are functors in the category
of vector spaces over
{\boldsymbol{F}}
and
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-linear maps. We need the fact
that these are indeed functors in the category of vector spaces over

{\boldsymbol{F}}
and
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
semi
linear maps. Since the pertinent facts seem less well known, we take the liberty
to expand some of the details, in the following.

Let
V,W
be vector spaces over
{\boldsymbol{F}}
. An explicit construction of
the tensor product
{V\otimes W}
is obtained by factoring the free
vector space
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{(V\times W)}
with basis
{V\times W}
over
{\boldsymbol{F}}

modulo the subspace
R_{V,W}
generated by all expressions of the
form
(v,w)+(v,y)-(v,w+y)
,
(v,w)+(x,w)-(v+x,w)
,
(vc,w)-(v,w)c

and
(v,wc)-(v,w)c
where
v,x\in V
,
w,y\in W
and
c\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
,
see
[
20
, p. 262]
. As usual, we write
v\otimes w\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(v,w)+R_{V,W}
. The tensor
power
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{V}
can now be constructed inductively from

{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{0}}{V}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\boldsymbol{F}}
and
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k+1}}{V}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=V\otimes{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{V}
.

In order to extend the morphism part of
\bigotimes^{k}
and
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!k}}

to the category of vector spaces over
{\boldsymbol{F}}
and

{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
semi
linear maps we first consider semilinear maps

\lambda\colon V\to W
and
\mu\colon{X\to Y}
with the same
companion field homomorphism
{\varphi\colon F\to F}
. Mapping
(v,x)\in V\times X
to
(\lambda(v),\mu(x))
extends to an

{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\varphi
-semilinear map from
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{(V\times X)}

to
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{(W\times Y)}
which maps
R_{V,X}
into
R_{W,Y}
. Thus

(\lambda\otimes\mu)(v\otimes x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lambda(v)\otimes\mu(x)

defines an
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\varphi
-semilinear map

{\lambda\otimes\mu}\colon{V\otimes X}\to{W\otimes Y}
.

Proceeding inductively with

\mu={\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k-1}}{\lambda}\colon{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k-1}}{V}\to{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k-1}}{W}
, we obtain

{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\varphi
-semilinear maps

{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{\lambda}\colon{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{V}\to{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{W}\colon v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{k}\mapsto\lambda(v_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda(v_{k})

for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now consider
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-semilinear maps
\lambda\colon V\to W
and

\mu\colon W\to Y
with (possibly different) companion field
homomorphisms
\varphi
and
\psi
, respectively. Then

{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{\mu}\left({\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{\lambda}(v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{k})\right)=\mu(\lambda(v_{1}))\otimes\dots\otimes\mu(\lambda(v_{k}))={\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{(\mu\circ\lambda)}(v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{k})

shows
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{\mu}\circ{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{\lambda}={\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{(\mu\circ\lambda)}
, and functoriality of
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}
is
established. Combination of these functors yields a functor
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{\,}}

with
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{\,}}{V}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{V}
and

({\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{\,}}{\lambda})(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}t_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{k}}{\lambda}(t_{k})
.

Finally we consider the exterior algebra

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!k}}{V}
which is obtained as the
quotient of
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{\,}}{V}
modulo the two-sided ideal
S_{V}
generated by

\left\{{v\otimes v}\left|\vphantom{v\otimes vv\in V}\right.\,{v\in V}\right\}
, cf.
[
20
, p. 288]
. We write

v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{k})+S_{V}
as usual. For each
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-semilinear
map
\lambda\colon V\to W
we note that
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{\,}}{\lambda}(S_{V})
is
contained in
S_{W}
. Thus

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!k}}{\lambda}(v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lambda(v_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge\lambda(v_{k})
is well defined. Note
that we use the multiplication in
{\textstyle\bigotimes\nolimits^{\,}}{V}
only for the definition
of
S_{V}
.

1.7 Example
.

Assume
\dim{V}=n
and consider an
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-semilinear endomorphism

{\lambda\colon V\to V}
with companion field
endomorphism
\varphi
. Choose a basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
for
V
;
then
\lambda
is the composite
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\circ\hat{\varphi}

of some linear map
\lambda^{\prime}\colon V\to V
with the map

\hat{\varphi}\colon\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{k}x_{k}\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{k}\varphi(x_{k})
. It is well known (cf.
[
25
, Thm. 4.11]

or
[
8
, III § 8]
) that
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}\lambda^{\prime}
is just multiplication by

\det{\lambda^{\prime}}
. For each
s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
we now have

\left({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}{\hat{\varphi}}\right)(v_{1}s\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n})={(v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n})\varphi(s)}
and thus

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\left({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}{\lambda}\right)\left((v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n})s\right)&=&{({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}{\lambda^{\prime}}\circ{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}{\hat{\varphi}})\left(v_{1}s\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n}\right)}\\
&=&\left(v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n}\right)\,\varphi(s)\,(\det\lambda^{\prime})\,.\end{array}

1.8 The unitary group
.

The special unitary group
\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}
acts in a natural way both
on
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}

and on
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V^{\vee}}

and commutes with
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{h}\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}\to{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}^{\vee}

because
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}
is a functor. The group
\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}
also commutes with

\mathrm{Pf}\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}V\to{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V^{\vee}
because its elements
have determinant
1
. Therefore it centralizes the Hodge
operator
J
. More generally, the groups

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{GU}{(V,h)}&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=&\left\{{\lambda\in\mathrm{GL}{(V)}}\left|\vphantom{\lambda\in\mathrm{GL}{(V)}\exists\,r_{\lambda}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}\,\forall\,v,w\in V\colon h\left(\lambda(v),\lambda(w)\right)=r_{\lambda}\,h(v,w)}\right.\,{\exists\,r_{\lambda}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}\,\forall\,v,w\in V\colon h\left(\lambda(v),\lambda(w)\right)=r_{\lambda}\,h(v,w)}\right\}\text{ and}\\[4.30554pt]
\Gamma\mathrm{U}{(V,h)}&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=&\left\{{\lambda\in\Gamma\mathrm{L}{(V)}}\left|\vphantom{\lambda\in\Gamma\mathrm{L}{(V)}\exists\,r_{\lambda}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}\,\forall\,v,w\in V\colon h\left(\lambda(v),\lambda(w)\right)=r_{\lambda}\,\varphi_{\lambda}(h(v,w))}\right.\,{\exists\,r_{\lambda}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}\,\forall\,v,w\in V\colon h\left(\lambda(v),\lambda(w)\right)=r_{\lambda}\,\varphi_{\lambda}(h(v,w))}\right\}\end{array}

of similitudes and semi-similitudes, respectively, also act
naturally on
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\,}}{V}
; here
\varphi_{\lambda}
denotes the companion
field automorphism of
\lambda\in\Gamma\mathrm{L}{(V)}
. A general element
\lambda
of
\Gamma\mathrm{U}{(V,h)}
will not centralize the
Hodge operator but it will normalize the set
{\boldsymbol{F}}\mathrm{id}\circ J
; in fact, the conjugate

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}\lambda\circ J\circ({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}\lambda)^{-1}=t_{\lambda}\mathrm{id}\circ J
, with

t_{\lambda}=\frac{\varphi_{\lambda}(\det\lambda^{\prime})}{r_{\lambda}^{\ell}}\,\frac{b({v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n}})}{\varphi_{\lambda}(b({v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n}}))}\,

where
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
is an orthonormal basis used to obtain a
decomposition
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\circ\hat{\varphi}
as
in
1.7
.
(This simplifies to
t_{\lambda}=r_{\lambda}^{-\ell}\,\det\lambda

if
\lambda
is
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-linear.)

Proof.

Only the assertion about

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}\lambda\circ J\circ({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}\lambda)^{-1}
remains to
be verified. Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
be the orthogonal basis that was used for the
decomposition
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\circ\hat{\varphi}
as
in
1.7
, and put
w_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lambda(v_{i})
. Then

w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
is an orthogonal basis, and

({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}\lambda)^{-1}(w_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge w_{\ell})=v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{\ell}
. Using
1.5
, we obtain that
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}\lambda\circ J\circ({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}\lambda)^{-1}
maps
{w_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge w_{\ell}}
to

\begin{array}[]{rcr}\left({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}\lambda\circ J\right)({v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{\ell}})&=&\left({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n-\ell}}\lambda\right)\left(v_{\ell+1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n}\,\frac{h(v_{1},v_{1})\cdots h(v_{\ell},v_{\ell})}{b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})}\,(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\right)\\
&=&(w_{\ell+1}\wedge\dots\wedge w_{n})\quad\varphi_{\lambda}\left(\frac{h(v_{1},v_{1})\cdots h(v_{\ell},v_{\ell})}{b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})}\right)\,(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\,.\end{array}

Now
h(w_{i},w_{i})=h(\lambda(v_{i}),\lambda(v_{i}))=r_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}(h(v_{i},v_{i}))
yields
\varphi_{\lambda}(h(v_{i},v_{i}))=r_{\lambda}^{-1}h(w_{i},w_{i})
. From
1.7
we know

{w_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge w_{n}}=({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}{\lambda})({v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}})={v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n}}\det{\lambda^{\prime}}
. So

\varphi_{\lambda}\bigl(b({v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}})\bigr)=\varphi_{\lambda}\bigl(b({w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{n}})(\det\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\bigr)
, and the assertion follows.
∎

1.9 Application in 3D
.

Let
h
be the standard bilinear form on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
; i.e., a form with an ortho
normal
basis

v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
. Moreover, let
b(v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3})=-1
. Then
J(v_{1})=-v_{2}\wedge v_{3}
,

J(v_{2})=v_{1}\wedge v_{3}
,
J(v_{3})=-v_{1}\wedge v_{2}
,
see
1.5
. In other words:

J\left(\begin{matrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-z&y\\
z&0&-x\\
-y&x&0\end{matrix}\right)

and this map is
\mathrm{SO}_{3}{(\mathbb{R})}
-equivariant, while

\lambda\circ J\circ\lambda^{-1}=-J
holds for each

\lambda\in\mathrm{O\kern 0.0pt}(3,\mathbb{R})\smallsetminus\mathrm{SO}_{3}{(\mathbb{R})}
.

Let

\gamma\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\colon(x,y,z)^{{}^{\intercal}}\mapsto(\gamma_{1}(x,y,z),\gamma_{2}(x,y,z),\gamma_{3}(x,y,z))^{{}^{\intercal}}
be a vector
field defined on some domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then the Hodge operator maps
\operatorname{curl}\gamma=({\partial_{y}\gamma_{3}-\partial_{z}\gamma_{2},\partial_{z}\gamma_{1}-\partial_{x}\gamma_{3},\partial_{x}\gamma_{2}-\partial_{y}\gamma_{1}})^{{}^{\intercal}}
to the anti-symmetrization of the Jacobian

\operatorname{Jac}\gamma
; indeed

\operatorname{Jac}\gamma-(\operatorname{Jac}\gamma)^{{}^{\intercal}}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\partial_{y}\gamma_{1}-\partial_{x}\gamma_{2}&\partial_{z}\gamma_{1}-\partial_{x}\gamma_{3}\\
\partial_{x}\gamma_{2}-\partial_{y}\gamma_{1}&0&\partial_{z}\gamma_{2}-\partial_{y}\gamma_{3}\\
\partial_{x}\gamma_{3}-\partial_{z}\gamma_{1}&\partial_{y}\gamma_{3}-\partial_{z}\gamma_{2}&0\end{matrix}\right)=J\left(\begin{matrix}\partial_{y}\gamma_{3}-\partial_{z}\gamma_{2}\\
\partial_{z}\gamma_{1}-\partial_{x}\gamma_{3}\\
\partial_{x}\gamma_{2}-\partial_{y}\gamma_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\,.

1.10 The square of the Hodge operator
.

Let
H
be the Gram matrix of
h
with respect to the basis

v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. The square of
J
is a linear automorphism
of
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
, and we find that

\textstyle J^{2}=\delta_{\ell}\,\mathrm{id}\quad\text{ where }\quad\delta_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\frac{\det(H)}{N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}(b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}))}\,.

Recall that
\det(H)
depends on the choice of basis; the invariant
would be
\operatorname{disc}(h)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}/N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}})
, the norm
class of
\det(h(v_{i},v_{j}))
. However, the whole expression depends only on
h
and
b
. Replacing
the isomorphism
b\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}V\to{\boldsymbol{F}}
changes
J
by a factor
and
J^{2}
by the norm of that factor. In particular, the isomorphism
type of the algebra
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
introduced in
2.1
below
does not depend on the choice of
b
. If
b
is chosen in such a way
that
b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})=1
, then we arrive at the textbook
formula
J^{2}=(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\det(H)\,\mathrm{id}
, cp.

[
37
, 6.1]
.

1.11 Lemma
.

For all
x,y\in{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
we have

a.

\mathrm{Pf}(J(x),y)={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)
,

b.

\mathrm{Pf}(x,J(y))=(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\,{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
,

c.

\mathrm{Pf}(J(x),J(y))=\delta_{\ell}\,{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
,

d.

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(J(x),y)=\delta_{\ell}\,{\mathrm{Pf}(x,y)}
,

e.

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,J(y))=\delta_{\ell}\,{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
,

f.

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(J(x),J(y))=(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\,\delta_{\ell}\,{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
.

Proof.

We compute

\mathrm{Pf}(J(x),-)=(\mathrm{Pf}\circ\mathrm{Pf}^{-1}\circ{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h})(x,-)={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,-)
and

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(J(x),-)=({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}\circ\mathrm{Pf}^{-1}\circ{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h})(x,-)=(\mathrm{Pf}\circ\mathrm{Pf}^{-1}\circ{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}\circ\mathrm{Pf}^{-1}\circ{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h})(x,-)=\mathrm{Pf}(J^{2}(x),-)={\delta_{\ell}\,\mathrm{Pf}(x,-)}
.
This gives the
assertions
a
and
d
, the rest follows from

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(y,x)={\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(x,y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
,

\mathrm{Pf}(y,x)=(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}\,\mathrm{Pf}(x,y)
and
J^{2}=\delta_{\ell}\,\mathrm{id}
.
∎

2
Composition algebras generated by Hodge operators

From now on, assume
n=2\ell
. Then
J
gives an

{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\sigma
-semilinear endomorphism

\textstyle J\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V\to{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V.

We are going to use
J
to give
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}
the structure of a right
module over an associative algebra of dimension
2\,\dim_{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{F}}
over
{\boldsymbol{R}}
.
Note that
(-1)^{(n-\ell)\ell}=(-1)^{\ell}
holds because
n=2\ell
.

2.1 The algebra
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
.

Let

\delta_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(-1)^{\ell}\frac{\det(H)}{N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}(b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}))}

as in
1.10
and let
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
denote the
{\boldsymbol{R}}
-algebra
consisting of all matrices of the form

\textstyle x=\begin{pmatrix}x_{0}&\delta_{\ell}\,{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{1}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\\
x_{1}&{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\end{pmatrix}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}^{2\times 2}.

We identify
x_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
with the diagonal matrix

\begin{pmatrix}x_{0}&0\\
0&{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\end{pmatrix}
and put

\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta_{\ell}\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
. Thus

\begin{pmatrix}x_{0}&\delta_{\ell}\,{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{1}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\\
x_{1}&{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\end{pmatrix}=x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1}\,.

We also have
\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}^{2}=\delta_{\ell}
and

\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x={\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}
for each
x\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
. Note
\dim_{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}=2\,\dim_{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{F}}
. For
x_{0},x_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
we put

\kappa(x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}-\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1}
. The map
\kappa
is called the
standard involution

of
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
. We also write
{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\kappa(x)
for
x\in{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
. As
\kappa
extends the action of
\sigma
on
{\boldsymbol{F}}\cong\left\{{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{0}&0\\
0&{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\left|\vphantom{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{0}&0\\
0&{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)x_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}}\right.\,{x_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}}\right\}
, this should cause no confusion.

We write
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\boldsymbol{F}}\smallsetminus\{0\}
for the
multiplicative group,
{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.21pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\displaystyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\textstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\scriptstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.6pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.18pt\hbox{$\kern-1.6pt\scriptscriptstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.5pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{s^{2}}\left|\vphantom{s^{2}s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}}\right.\,{s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}}\right\}
, and

{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.21pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\displaystyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\textstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\scriptstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.6pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.18pt\hbox{$\kern-1.6pt\scriptscriptstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.5pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.21pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\displaystyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\textstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\scriptstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.6pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.18pt\hbox{$\kern-1.6pt\scriptscriptstyle{\phantom{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}}\kern-1.5pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Then

{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}/{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.21pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\displaystyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\textstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\scriptstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.6pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.18pt\hbox{$\kern-1.6pt\scriptscriptstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.5pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}}
is the group of non-trivial square classes
of
{\boldsymbol{F}}
; note that this is an elementary abelian
2
-group.

2.2 Lemma
.

The standard involution is an anti-automorphism of
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}

satisfying
\kappa(x)\,x=x\,\kappa(x)=\det_{\boldsymbol{F}}\left(x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1}\right)=N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}(x_{0})-\delta_{\ell}\,N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}(x_{1})\in{\boldsymbol{R}}
for every

x={x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1}}\in{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
. Thus the determinant gives a multiplicative quadratic form

\det\colon{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}\to{\boldsymbol{R}}
. The corresponding polarization
f_{\det}

is degenerate precisely if
{\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2}
and
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
; indeed, it vanishes in that case. The quadratic form
\det
is then degenerate if, and only if, the
bilinear form
h
has discriminant
1\in{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}/{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.21pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\displaystyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule\vphantom{x}\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\textstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.7pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.25pt\hbox{$\kern-1.7pt\scriptstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.6pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\hline\cr\cr\vrule$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{x}$\smash{\lower 0.18pt\hbox{$\kern-1.6pt\scriptscriptstyle{{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}}\kern-1.5pt$}}\vrule\crcr\cr\hline\cr}}}}
.

Proof.

Standard matrix computations suffice to verify that
\kappa
is
indeed an anti-automorphism, with the properties as claimed. The value of the polar form at

(x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1},y_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,y_{1})
is obtained as
\det_{\boldsymbol{F}}\left(x_{0}+y_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}(x_{1}+y_{1})\right)-\det_{\boldsymbol{F}}\left(x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1}\right)-\det_{\boldsymbol{F}}\left(y_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,y_{1}\right)=x_{0}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{y_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{y_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{y_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{y_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}+y_{0}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}-\delta_{\ell}(x_{1}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{y_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{y_{1}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{y_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}+y_{1}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{1}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}}\hfil$\crcr}}}})
. Clearly, this polar form is zero if
{\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2}

and
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
.

If
\sigma\neq\mathrm{id}
we pick
s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}\smallsetminus{\boldsymbol{R}}
with
s+{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}=1
;
then
s{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}=s-s^{2}
. With respect to the basis
(1,0)
,
(s,0)
,

(0,1)
,
(0,s)
, the Gram matrix of the polar form becomes

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1&0&0\\
1&2s{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}&0&0\\
0&0&-2\delta_{\ell}&-\delta_{\ell}\\
0&0&-\delta_{\ell}&-2\delta_{\ell}s{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right),

with determinant

\delta_{\ell}^{2}(4s{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}-1)^{2}=\delta_{\ell}^{2}(1-2s)^{2}\neq 0
.

If
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
then the Gram matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&0\\
0&-2\delta_{\ell}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for the polar form is nonsingular precisely if

\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}\neq 2
.

Finally, assume
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
and
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}\neq 2
. Then
h
has
discriminant
1
precisely if
\delta_{\ell}
is a square. In that
case, the quadratic form

\det_{\boldsymbol{F}}(x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1})=x_{0}^{2}-\delta_{\ell}x_{1}^{2}=(x_{0}-\sqrt{\delta_{\ell}}x_{1})^{2}
is isotropic, and then degenerate
because its polar form is zero.
∎

2.3 Remarks
.

The isomorphism type of
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
as an
{\boldsymbol{R}}
-algebra does not depend
on the choice of the orthogonal basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, and does not
depend on the choice of
b
; cp. the discussion
following
1.10
. In the sequel, we will choose the basis in such a way that the Gram
matrix
H
has a convenient determinant, and then usually adapt
b

such that
b(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n})=1
; then
\delta_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell}\det H
.

If the polar form
f_{\det}
is non-degenerate then
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
is
indeed a
composition algebra
, cf.
[
29
, 1.5.1]

or
[
18
, § 7.6]
. In this case the standard
involution
\kappa
is uniquely determined by the properties of
being an
{\boldsymbol{R}}
-linear anti-automorphism of
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
and
inducing
-\mathrm{id}
on
{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\perp}
. As such, it is rightfully termed “standard”. Note that

{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\perp}=\{{x\in{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}\smallsetminus{\boldsymbol{R}}}\left|\vphantom{}\right.\,{x^{2}\in{\boldsymbol{R}}}\}\cup\{0\}
if

\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{R}}\neq 2
, and
{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\perp}=\{{x\in{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}}\left|\vphantom{}\right.\,{x^{2}\in{\boldsymbol{R}}}\}

if
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{R}}=2
. The norm form of the composition algebra
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
is a Pfister
form; it is the orthogonal sum of the norm form
N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}
and its
scalar multiple
-\delta_{\ell}N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}
.

If
f_{\det}
is degenerate we have
\kappa=\mathrm{id}
and

\det(x)=x{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}=x^{2}
. These inseparable cases will be discussed
in
[
23
]
in greater detail.

Note that
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
contains non-invertible elements different
from
0
if, and only if, the factor
\delta_{\ell}
is a norm; i.e.,
if
(-1)^{\ell}
lies in
N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}({\boldsymbol{F}})
. In that case, we call the
algebra
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}

split
; we may (and will) normalize

\delta_{\ell}=1
then. The case where
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
is split will be discussed in
Section
3
below. In particular, the existence of
idempotents or of nilpotent elements in

{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}\smallsetminus\{0,1\}
leads to the existence of certain

\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}
-submodules in
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
,
see
3.3
below.

If
\delta_{\ell}
is not a norm then
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}|{\boldsymbol{F}}
is either a
quadratic extension (for
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
; that extension is inseparable
if
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2
) or a quaternion division algebra over
{\boldsymbol{R}}
(for

\sigma\neq\mathrm{id}
). See Section
7
below.

2.4 Proposition
.

Via conjugation in
\mathrm{End}_{{\boldsymbol{R}}}({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V)
, every similitude
of
h
induces an automorphism of the
{\boldsymbol{R}}
-subalgebra
M_{\ell}
generated by
J
and
{\boldsymbol{F}}\,\mathrm{id}
in
\mathrm{End}_{{\boldsymbol{R}}}({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V)
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma
be a similitude with multiplier
r
. Then

\gamma\circ J\circ\gamma^{-1}=Jr^{-\ell}\det\gamma
. Using a
matrix
A
describing
\gamma
and the Gram matrix
H
for
h
with
respect to the same basis, we note that
{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{A}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}}{{}^{\intercal}}HA=rH
,
and infer
N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}(\det A)=r^{2\ell}
. So

N_{{\boldsymbol{F}}|{\boldsymbol{R}}}(r^{-\ell}\det\gamma)=1
, and a straightforward
calculation yields that conjugation by
\gamma
yields an algebra
automorphism, as claimed.
∎

Obviously, the algebra
M_{\ell}
is anti-isomorphic to
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
, and
then in fact isomorphic because
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
admits an anti-automorphism.

2.5 Definition
.

For
v\in{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
we put

v\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=J(v)
.

In this way,
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
becomes a
\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}
-
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
-bimodule,
i.e., it becomes a right module over
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
and
\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}
acts

{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
-linearly from the left. Choosing an orthogonal basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
for
V
with a fixed
ordering we obtain a basis
B
for
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
consisting of all

v_{j_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{j_{\ell}}
where
(j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell})
is
an increasing sequence of length
\ell
in
\{1,\dots,n\}
. The
sequences with
j_{1}=1
form a subset
B_{1}
of
B
, and
J
maps each
element of
B_{1}
to one of
B\smallsetminus B_{1}
. Moreover, the
set
B_{1}
forms a basis for the
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
-module
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
, showing that
the latter is a free module.

2.6 The
\alpha
-hermitian form
.

We define
g\colon{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}\times{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}\to{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
by

\textstyle g(u,v)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(u,v)+{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(u,v\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell})\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}^{-1}={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(u,v)+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,(-1)^{\ell}\,\mathrm{Pf}(u,v)\,;

see
1.11
for the description on the right hand
side. This expression is
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-linear in the right argument (i.e.,

g(u,vs)=g(u,v)s
), and
{\boldsymbol{F}}
-
\sigma
-semilinear in the left argument
(in the sense that
g(us,v)={\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}g(u,v)
for all
s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
and all

{u,v\in{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}}
). Moreover,
g(u,v\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell})={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(u,v\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell})+{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(u,(v\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell})\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell})\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}^{-1}={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(u,v\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell})+{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(u,v)\,\delta_{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}^{-1}g(u,v)\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}
, so
g
is indeed
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
-linear in the right argument. Let
\alpha

denote the following involution:

\alpha(x_{0}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x_{0}}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x_{0}}\hfil$\crcr}}}}+(-1)^{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}\,x_{1}\,.

Then
\alpha
is an
{\boldsymbol{R}}
-linear anti-automorphism of
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
, with

\alpha(x)={\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
for
x\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
and
\alpha(\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell})=(-1)^{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}
. Using the fact that
\mathrm{Pf}
is graded symmetric we find
g(v,u)=\alpha(g(u,v))
. This shows that
g
is an
\alpha
-hermitian form on

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
.

Note that
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
is not a field, in general: we need the more
general concept of hermitian forms over rings (e.g.,
see
[
22
]
).

2.7 Proposition
.

The form
g
is diagonalizable.

Proof.

In fact, for any orthogonal basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
of
V
, consider
the vector
w_{\gamma}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=v_{1}\wedge v_{\gamma_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{\gamma_{\ell}}
for each increasing sequence

\gamma=(\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{\ell})
. Then the
w_{\gamma}
form a
basis for the free
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
-module

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
. By
1.5
, we have

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}h(w_{\gamma},w_{\beta})=0
if
\gamma\neq\beta
, and

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}h(w_{\gamma},w_{\gamma})=h(v_{1},v_{1})h(v_{\gamma_{2}},v_{\gamma_{2}})\dots h(v_{\gamma_{\ell}},v_{\gamma_{\ell}})
for each admissible
\gamma
.

As every
w_{\gamma}
is a wedge product starting with the same
vector
v_{1}
, we have
\mathrm{Pf}(w_{\gamma},w_{\beta})=0
, and

g(w_{\gamma},w_{\beta})={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}h(w_{\gamma},w_{\beta})
.
∎

2.8 Remarks
.

The involution
\alpha
coincides with the standard involution

\kappa
if
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2
or if
\ell
is odd. If
\sigma\neq\mathrm{id}
and

\ell
is even we pick any
a\in{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\vphantom{x}\smash{\times}}
with
{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{a}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{a}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{a}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{a}\hfil$\crcr}}}}=-a
and
obtain
\alpha(x)=a^{-1}\kappa(x)a
for all
x\in{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}
. If
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
and
\ell
is even then
\alpha=\mathrm{id}
. The idea of (re-)constructing the hermitian form
g
dates back
to
[
17
, p. 266]
, cf.
[
31
, 4.4]

and
[
4
, 2.9]
.

2.9 Lemma
.

a.

For
X,Y\in{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{V}
we have

g(X,Y)=0\iff\mathrm{Pf}(X,Y)=0={\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(X,Y)\,.

In particular, the form
g
is not degenerate (since
h
is not
degenerate, see
1.5
).

b.

If
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}
is anisotropic then
g
is anisotropic,
too.

c.

If
h
is isotropic then both
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}h
and
g
are
isotropic, as well.

Proof.

Comparing coefficients in
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}={\boldsymbol{F}}\oplus\boldsymbol{j}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{F}}
we obtain the
first assertion. The second one follows immediately.

The form
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!1}}h
coincides with
h
. For
n=\dim{V}
the
form
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}{h}
on
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}V\cong{\boldsymbol{F}}^{1}
will be anisotropic,
regardless of what the (non-degenerate) form
h
may be.

So consider
2\leq\ell<n
, and assume that
h
is isotropic. Then
V
is the orthogonal sum of an anisotropic space and a
non-trivial split space
S
, see
[
28
, III, (1.1), p. 56]
. In the split space
S
, we find
x,y
such that
h(x,x)=0
and

h(x,y)=1
, see
[
28
, I, (6.3), p. 56]
.

If
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}\neq 2
, we may also assume
h(y,y)=0
. We then put

v_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=x+y
and
v_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=x-y
, and extend to an
orthogonal basis
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}
for
V
.

If
\operatorname{char}{\boldsymbol{F}}=2
, there are two possibilities: either the restriction
of
h
to
S
is alternating, or it is possible to choose
x,y\in S
such that

h(x,x)=0\neq h(y,y)
and
h(x,y)=1
. In the first case, we find
u\in S^{\perp}\smallsetminus\{0\}
because the
(diagonalizable and non-degenerate) form
h
is not alternating. In the second case, we just take
u=0
. We now put
v_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=y+u
and
v_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=xh(v_{1},v-y_{1})
. Then
h(v_{2},v_{2})=h(v_{1},v_{1})\neq 0
, and
h(v_{1},v_{2})=0
. So there
exists an orthogonal basis
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}
for
V
.

If
\ell=2
, put
w_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=v_{1}\wedge v_{3}
and
w_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=v_{2}\wedge v_{3}
. If
\ell>2
, put
w_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=v_{1}\wedge v_{3}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{\ell+1}
and
w_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=v_{2}\wedge v_{3}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{\ell+1}
. Then
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(w_{1},w_{1})=-{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(w_{2},w_{2})
yields

{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}(w_{1}+w_{2},w_{1}+w_{2})=0
, so
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}
is
isotropic.

Finally, recall from
1.2
that

\mathrm{Pf}(w_{1}+w_{2},w_{1}+w_{2})=0
, so the form
g
is isotropic, as well.
∎

2.10 Remarks
.

It may happen that
h
is anisotropic but
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}{h}
becomes
isotropic. For instance, this happens whenever we start with an
anisotropic bilinear form
h
on a vector space of dimension
4

over a
p
-adic field because there are no anisotropic quadratic
forms in more than four variables over such a field
(see
[
24
, VI 2.2]
).

For more explicit examples, take
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
as the
polarizations of the quadratic forms
x_{0}^{2}-2x_{1}^{2}+3x_{2}^{2}-6x_{3}^{2}

and
x_{0}^{2}+2x_{1}^{2}+10x_{3}^{2}-5x_{4}^{2}
, respectively, over
\mathbb{Q}
. Both forms are anisotropic; the discriminant of
h_{1}
is a square
while that of
h_{2}
is not. Both
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!2}}{h_{1}}
and
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!2}}{h_{2}}
are isotropic. For
h_{2}
, the corresponding form
g
is isotropic (over

{\boldsymbol{K}}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-1})
), as well,
see
8.1
below.

2.11 The homomorphism
.

From the definition of
g
it is clear that
\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}

preserves
g
and that
\Gamma\mathrm{U}{(V,h)}
acts by semi-similitudes of
g
,
see
1.8
. Thus we have, for
\dim(V)=n=2\ell
, constructed a homomorphism
\eta_{\ell}\colon\Gamma\mathrm{U}{(V,h)}\to\Gamma\mathrm{U}{({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V,g)}
which restricts to

\eta_{\ell}|_{\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}}\colon\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)}\to\mathrm{U}{({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V,g)}\,.

Of course, we have to read
\Gamma\mathrm{U}{(V,h)}
as
\mathrm{\Gamma O}{(V,h)}
if

\sigma=\mathrm{id}
, and
\Gamma\mathrm{U}{({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V,g)}
as
\mathrm{\Gamma O}{({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V,g)}
if

\sigma=\mathrm{id}
and
\ell
is even.

2.12 Lemma
.

The kernel of
\eta_{\ell}
consists of all scalar multiples

s\,\mathrm{id}
of the identity where
{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}s=1=s^{\ell}
. The center of the image
\eta_{\ell}(\mathrm{SU}{(V,h)})
has
order at most
2
because
1=\det(s\,\mathrm{id})
already implies

s^{n}=s^{2\ell}=1
.

Proof.

Via
(v_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{\ell}){\boldsymbol{F}}\mapsto v_{1}{\boldsymbol{F}}+\dots+v_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{F}}

we identify
\mathrm{Gr}_{1,{\boldsymbol{F}}}({\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V)
with
\mathrm{Gr}_{\ell,{\boldsymbol{F}}}(V)
. If some

A\in\Gamma\mathrm{L}{(V)}
with companion
\mu_{A}
fixes each element
of
\mathrm{Gr}_{\ell,{\boldsymbol{F}}}(V)
then it also fixes each element of
\mathrm{Gr}_{1,{\boldsymbol{F}}}(V)

because these are obtained as intersections of
\ell
-dimensional
spaces. As
{\boldsymbol{F}}
is commutative, we find (by evaluating
A
on a basis
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
and at
b_{1}+\cdots+b_{n}t

for
t\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
, see
[
3
, III.1, Prop. 3(b), p. 43]
) that
there exists
s\in{\boldsymbol{F}}
such that
A=s\,\mathrm{id}
and
ts=s\mu_{A}(t)
. In
particular,
A
is linear. Now
A
is trivial on
{\textstyle\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!\ell}}V
precisely if
s^{\ell}=1
. The rest is clear.
∎

2.13 Example:
\mathrm{S\alpha U}_{n}{(\mathbb{H})}
.

Let
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{R}+i\mathbb{R}+j\mathbb{R}+k\mathbb{R}
be the (essentially unique)
quaternion division algebra over
\mathbb{R}
. Then
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}+i\mathbb{R}
is the
field of complex numbers, and we obtain
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{C}+j\mathbb{C}
. Composing
the standard involution
\kappa\colon a\mapsto{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{a}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{a}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{a}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{a}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
with the
inner automorphism
a\mapsto i^{-1}ai=-iai
, we obtain the
involutory anti-automorphism
\alpha\colon x+jy\mapsto{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}+jy

(here
x,y\in\mathbb{C}
). On
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, we now define the
\alpha
-hermitian form
f\colon\mathbb{H}^{n}\times\mathbb{H}^{n}\to\mathbb{H}
by
f\bigl((a_{1},\dots,a_{n}),(b_{1},\dots b_{n})\bigr)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\alpha(a_{1})\,b_{1}+\cdots+\alpha(a_{n})\,b_{n}
.
The corresponding unitary group is denoted by
\mathrm{S\alpha U}_{n}{(\mathbb{H},f)}
, its
Lie algebra by
\mathsf{D}_{n}^{\mathbb{H}}
(in the notation of Tits
[
33
]
; Helgason
[
15
]
denotes the corresponding Lie algebras
by
\mathfrak{so}^{*}(2n)
). We also note that the form
f
can be replaced by the skew-hermitian
form
if
, without changing the unitary group (but changing the
involution back to the standard one).

2.14 Example
.

An interesting special case of our construction occurs if
{\boldsymbol{F}}=\mathbb{C}
,

{\boldsymbol{R}}=\mathbb{R}
,
\ell=2
, and
h
is a hermitian form of Witt index
1

on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
. The algebra
{\boldsymbol{K}}
is then isomorphic to
\mathbb{H}
, the
involution
\alpha
has a
3
-dimensional space of fixed points, and
is thus a conjugate of the involution used in
2.13
. Our
result
2.11
thus provides a covering

\mathrm{SU}_{4}{(\mathbb{C},1)}\to\mathrm{S\alpha U}_{3}{(\mathbb{H},g)}
corresponding to the isomorphism
from the Lie algebra
\mathsf{A}_{3}^{\mathbb{C},1}
onto
\mathsf{D}_{3}^{\mathbb{H}}
(in
the notation of Tits
[
33
]
; Helgason
[
15
]
denotes the corresponding Lie algebras
by
\mathfrak{su}(3,1)
and
\mathfrak{so}^{*}(6)
, respectively).

The group
\mathrm{U}_{4}{(\mathbb{C},1)}
is a semidirect product of its commutator
group
\mathrm{SU}_{4}{(\mathbb{C},1)}
with a group isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. With respect to a basis
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
with

h(v_{1},v_{1})=h(v_{2},v_{2})=h(v_{3},v_{3})=1
and
h(v_{4},v_{4})=-1
, we
consider the linear maps
\gamma_{s}
defined by
\gamma_{s}(v_{1})=v_{1}
,

\gamma_{s}(v_{2})=v_{2}
,
\gamma_{s}(v_{3})=v_{3}
, and
\gamma_{s}(v_{4})=v_{4}s
,
with
s{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}=1
. Then
\left\{{\gamma_{s}}\left|\vphantom{\gamma_{s}s\in\mathbb{C},s{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}=1}\right.\,{s\in\mathbb{C},s{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{s}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{s}\hfil$\crcr}}}}=1}\right\}
is a
complement for
\mathrm{SU}_{4}{(\mathbb{C},1)}
in
\mathrm{U}_{4}{(\mathbb{C},1)}
. It turns out that
\eta_{2}(\gamma_{s})
is an
\mathbb{H}
-semilinear map with
companion
\varphi_{s}\colon x+\boldsymbol{j}y\mapsto x+\boldsymbol{j}sy

(see
2.4
), fixing each vector in the

\mathbb{H}
-basis
v_{1}\wedge v_{2}
,
v_{1}\wedge v_{3}
,
v_{2}\wedge v_{3}
.

We also remark that the
\mathbb{C}
-semilinear map
\lambda
with
companion
\sigma
and fixing each
v_{k}
induces the

\mathbb{H}
-semilinear map
\eta_{2}(\lambda)
with companion

\varphi\colon x+\boldsymbol{j}y\mapsto{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{x}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}}+\boldsymbol{j}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{y}$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 1.0pt\cr\kern 1.0pt\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
fixing each one of

v_{1}\wedge v_{2}
,
v_{1}\wedge v_{3}
,
v_{2}\wedge v_{3}
.

3
The split cases

We will call
